@@135861968 <h> 5 Sci-Fi Books From the 1990s That Are Unquestionable 5 Star Classics <p> The 1990s were the best decade , not just in general but for a wide range of types of entertainment . Television , movies , and even video games just hit different in the 1990s , giving fans iconic stories and characters that stick with us even now . When it comes to specific genres , sci-fi in particular was thriving in the 1990s as well with some genuine masterpieces of movies and television that still hold up . <h> Videos by ComicBook.com <p> But movies , television , and video games were n't the only types of entertainment that were thriving in the 1990s . Books , particularly sci-fi books , were also truly great during the decade . While sci-fi books are currently having another great era thanks to some new and upcoming books , the 1990s should n't be discounted as a great sci-fi decade for readers . Here are five books from the decade that are unquestionable classics -- and you should read them if you somehow have n't already . <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> While it might be hard to distance The Giver from required school reading if you happened to be a kid growing up in the 1990s or 2000s , the truth is that Lois Lowry 's 1993 novel is unquestionably one of the best sci-fi novels for young adults ever written -- and it holds up beautifully even if you 're an adult reader . The first book in a very loose quartet of novels set in the same universe ( Gathering Blue , Messenger , and Son are the other three ) , The Giver is set in a society that , on the surface , appears to be a utopia as the society has eliminated pain and suffering and has complete equality having converted to something called " Sameness " . One person , the Receiver of Memory , stores all of the memories of the time before this Sameness . When a 12-year-old boy is chosen to become the new Receiver of Memory , he struggles with emotions as he is introduced to them and the society is slowly revealed to be dystopian . <p> The Giver @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but it also has some fascinating and deep themes . Memory and religion are key themes in the book as is the idea of the absence of color being a key part of Sameness . There are also questions about eugenics and government control of the population that come up in the book , making The Giver complex and layered . It 's a masterpiece . <h> 4 ) The Sparrow by Mary Doria Russell ( 1996 ) <p> If we 're being honest , both The Sparrow and its 1998 sequel , Children of God , belong on this list so consider this entry a two-for-one . Set in the then-future of 2019 , The Sparrow follows Emilio Sandoz , a Jesuit priest who is part of a mission to make first contact with extraterrestrial life after the SETI program discovers radio broadcasts of music from Rakhat , a world area near Alpha Centauri . Over 40 years later , only Emilio returns , the sole survivor of the journey , broken in body and mind . <p> The Sparrow is , in many ways , a harrowing book @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plots that follow Emilio 's space journey and his return to Earth , the novel takes a deep look about faith , evil , what it means to be human , and how one 's faith handles -- or does not handle -- the unknown when truly faced with it . Part sci-fi , part horror , it 's a great book . The follow up , Children of God , continues the story as the Jesuits return to Rakhat and is a great book as well . <h> 3 ) Use of Weapons by Iain M. Banks ( 1990 ) <p> While Use of Weapons is the third novel in Banks ' Culture series , you do n't have to have read the previous books to enjoy this one or even recognize why it 's considered a near perfect sci-fi novel by many readers . The book is set up as though it 's the biography of a man named Cheradenine Zakalwe , born outside of the Culture , but recruited into it by Diziet Sma , a Special Circumstances agent , to work as an operative in less @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fully describe Use of Weapons -- it 's one of those books that you just have to read -- but the book has a theme that is pretty straightforward : it 's about weapons and how they are used , but it also include people as weapons . Zakalwe is being used as a weapon by the people he works for even as he is using actual weapons . The book has strong themes of disillusionment and consequence . Not the easiest of reads , but very well worth the time . <h> 2 ) Jurassic Park by Michael Crichton ( 1990 ) <p> While many people only think about Jurassic Park in terms of the blockbuster movie franchise , this is your reminder that it was actually a science fiction novel first -- and one that does n't get nearly enough credit for how great it actually is . The novel is , as you probably can guess , about a theme park located on a remote island where dinosaurs are brought back from extinction thanks to genetic engineer . It seems cool , but when the park 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a terrifying fight for survival . <p> The book has some surprisingly sound science , but the thing about Jurassic Park is that it also makes you think about the idea of " just because we can , does that mean we should ? " Rather than being just a high-octane action thriller of a sci-fi , the novel is also a serious cautionary tale . Also worth noting that there are some key differences between the book and the movie ( the latter of which Crichton co-wrote ) so even if you think you know Jurassic Park , you still should check this out . <p> 1 ) Parable of the Sower by Octavia E. Butler ( 1993 ) <p> Parable of the Sower is one of those books that regularly pops up on " best of " sci-fi lists and for a good reason . It 's an incredible book . It also has a sequel , Parable of the Talents that was published in 1998 . Set on a post-apocalyptic earth that has been catastrophically impacted by climate change as well as social inequality , Parable of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ feel the pain of others . After she is displaced from her home , Lauren is joined on a journey north by various others with Lauren preaching of a religion that she has come up with called Earthseed . <p> Both a complex sci-fi and a layered meditation on society , Parable of the Sower is a haunting book . It 's follow up , Parable of the Talents , is also good . Unfortunately , while there was meant to be more books in the Parable series , Butler struggled with writers block later in life and died in 2006 before she was able to finish the series . Even with that , Parable of the Sower reads as complete in a standalone sense and is very well worth the time . <p> What do you think ? @@135863365 <p> Big increase : HIV infections are on the rise in Chicago after about two decades of decline , growing 29% between 2022 and 2024 . AIDS cases are also up slightly . In 2024 , there were 818 new HIV cases -- and nearly half were among Latinos , the first time the racial and ethnic group accounted for most new diagnoses , AIDS Foundation Chicago CEO John Peller said . <p> How we got here : Chicago health officials say one early clue behind the increase among Latinos is a change in migration patterns , which possibly include people who had n't been tested or maintained care . Ripple effects of the COVID-19 pandemic also could have interrupted HIV care . <p> Key context : The change comes amid two contradictory trends . Testing and treatment for HIV have advanced so much that it allows people with the virus who are treated to live relatively normal lives and , critically , not transmit the virus to anyone else .
